A lesser known fact about Nazi book burning is that a significant number of books that were burned were part of the library of Jewish sexologist Magnus Hirschfeld, whose Institute of Sexual Studies was the first clinic in the world to provide surgical interventions allowing trans people to live in the bodies they wanted. The institute opened in 1919 and provided care and sought to dispel shame around same-sex relationships and gender nonconformity, as well as publishing pathbreaking research into trans-affirming care. And nearly a century later, white nationalist horror at gender non-conformity and hatred towards trans people remains at the center of far-right movements in the United States. A post from July 18, 2021, on the neo-Nazi blog Occidental Observer by open white supremacist Andrew Joyce is an example of this. In the post, he attacked both Magnus Hirschfeld’s activism on behalf of LGBTQ individuals and a 1934 book that condemned racism in all its forms, saying “Love as a concept was altered and weaponized by Hirschfeld, who imbued it with transcendental and cosmic qualities in an effort to distance it as much as possible from biological, reproductive drives. Racism, homophobia, and transphobia, which together essentially boil down to the idea that Whites should be able to live normally and by themselves, are perceived today as beyond the sphere of this deified ‘love’ and are therefore representative of a kind of modern heresy.” Basically gayness, gender-nonconformity and anti-racism are seen as an attack on the existence of white people. This is a stark example of how racism and transphobia are intertwined in the modern white power movement. Gender nonconformity threatens their world view, one that is fixated on immutable and antiquated gender roles, where white women are seen as walking wombs that must be used to replenish the declining white birth rates and reverse replacement of the white race by nonwhite children. The desirable qualities in a woman are chastity, submission, and silence. And the vitriol and hate being spouted on far right neo-Nazi chatrooms and blogs is often reiterated and given validation by Fox News hosts, prominent influencers spouting disinformation and pushing the narrative that LGBTQ people are child groomers, like Libs Of TikTok, a twitter account with over 2 million followers, and via speeches by Donald Trump where he either blatantly espouses the same values, or gives Proud Boys a nod and a wink on national television. Writer and researcher Talia Lavin sums it up well in a recent article, writing “What the far right proclaims through explicit street violence it wants to reinforce with the implicit violence of the state. Bills that seek to suppress anti-racist education, anti-trans legislation that seeks to codify antiquated gender roles, and the severe and continual restriction of abortion rights in states with right-wing legislatures typify the ways hatred of gender nonconformity, a desire to control women’s bodies, and racism intermingle.” As a result of this, the last two years have been the deadliest for transgender people, especially black transgender women, with nearly one in five of all hate crimes motivated by anti-LGBTQ bias. Trans Americans are four times more likely to be victims of violent crime. 86% of trans or nonbinary youth reported in a 2022 survey that they experience negative effects on their mental health stemming from the political debate around trans issues, and nearly half had seriously considered suicide in the past year. Because, again, say it with me: it's never been about protecting children. So why are conservatives so obsessed with trans kids? You have to show up to vote, especially in local elections where this is happening. You have to get into the ear of your lawmakers, your city council members, your school board members. Be relentless. The power in the conservative movement, especially in the religious right, is that they’re active in local politics and they show up. So you need to show up, but do it more. **Structure:** Opens with a statistic (nearly 500 anti-trans bills introduced that year) and names the through-line, a small-population issue turned into a deliberate wedge strategy, then a sponsor read (Warby Parker), then a chronological/topical rundown of specific state bills and the political mechanics behind the strategy, closing with a call to keep showing up plus the standard Patreon/cross-promo sign-off.
**Framing:** Headline-collage framing: the thumbnail visually stacks several real news chyrons behind her so the video reads as a synthesis of the same week's news rather than a single-story reaction, then the opening stat immediately reframes the issue's scale against its political weight.

So claiming they are doing it to protect Jordanian civilians, and not to protect Israel, is more politically tenable. Jordan s involvement of course still angered Iran, which warned Israel s allies not to shoot down missiles and threatened that the conflict would spread to US bases in the region. On Sunday, the conflict continued with explosions across Israel and Iran, including attacks on Israel by Houthis from Yemen, the first Iranian ally to enter the fray. As of Monday, 224 Iranians had been killed with 24 killed in Israel, most of the dead were civilians, and millions of civilians have been affected throughout the region, with many forced into air raid shelters and advised to take cover or flee. Lines at gas stations and bumper to bumper traffic has been reported in Tehran as many attempt to leave the area or flee Iran altogether. On Monday, Iranian missiles hit Israel s largest oil refinery in Haifa Bay. Israel struck the offices of Iran s state broadcaster. Israel claims to have destroyed more than a third of Iran s missile launchers. On Tuesday, Israel carried out extensive strikes on Iranian military targets, and Iran claims to have hit Israel s Military Intelligence Directorate and the operation center of Israel s spy agency Mossad. And as of today, Wednesday June 18th, the International Atomic Energy Agency confirmed that Israel struck two centrifuge production facilities in Iran, and that there had been direct impacts on underground enrichment halls in Iran s largest uranium enrichment center in Natanz. Okay, where does the US fit into this. Back in 2015, Obama reached an agreement with Iran to limit its nuclear capabilities and require ongoing monitoring and reporting requirements. In 2018, Trump withdrew from the deal, complaining it wasn t tough enough. And then no new deal was ever reached. A new nuclear deal was to be negotiated on Sunday, again that got scrapped after Israel attacked Iran. Trump initially indicated he didn t want to get involved, but has since increased his rhetoric against Iran, calling for Iran s unconditional surrender and saying nobody knows if he will order US forces to attack Iran s nuclear sites. He literally said nobody knows what I m going to do. That s, like, NOT good. The US armed forces have shifted military aircraft and warships in recent days to locations around the Middle East, and US officials confirmed Tuesday that they have taken defensive actions to take out incoming Iranian missiles to protect Israel. Additional refueling aircraft and fighter jets have been moved to strategic locations in Europe, including England, Spain, Germany, and Greece, according to public aviation tracking websites. And US fighter jets have been patrolling the skies around the Middle East, allegedly to protect US personnel and installations on the ground. And amid all of these strategic moves and delicate tensions, you have Trump, a roving idiot with a social media addiction, making posts that are taking a sledgehammer to everything. In one post, he said we now have complete and total control of the skies over Iran. And Iran outright rejected Trump s social media calls for an unconditional surrender a phrase I m sure Trump has heard in a war documentary before but that actually means something and hasn t been requested from a country since World War 2. So as of right now, the US has posted up a large fleet of ships and aircraft, shot down missiles and drones headed for Israel, and vowed to protect its personnel on the ground in the region. A lot of attention has been paid to the fact that, if Israel wants to knock out Iran s nuclear capabilities, it would need to get at its nuclear site Fordo, a complex built deep inside a mountain. This is something Israel cannot do on its own. The only bomb capable of penetrating deep enough into the ground to hit Fordo is the 30,000 pound GBU-57 Massive Ordnance Penetrator, also known as the bunker buster. And the US is the only country that has one, and the US Air Force s B-2 Spirit stealth bomber is the only aircraft that can carry the 30,000 pound bunker buster. As of right now, there are no B-2s in the middle east, and Israel has been barred from obtaining a bunker buster. Absent the use of the bunker buster, Israel is attempting other measures to block entrance to Fordo or attack nearby energy centers, to try to render the site unusable. So that s where we stand now Israel is hitting various Iranian nuclear facilities, killing key commanders and nuclear scientists, along with civilians, and Iran is retaliating by hitting key Israeli assets. And the US is standing by and acting vaguely in defense of Israel without actually entering into the war by carrying out attacks of its own or supplying Israel with the bunker buster of its dreams. And nobody knows what Trump is going to do. So that s probably bad. Here are the mitigating factors I m holding on to. Trump is a fucking idiot with lead fingers ready to shoot off tweets at all hours of the night that could fully upend the current world order. That is true. And he has put in place leaders that are equally fucking stupid, like Pete Hegseth, Secretary of Defense, who is so woefully unqualified to be handling this shit it s actually shocking. But there are still people within the armed forces that have a grasp on middle east relationships and the severity of this situation. Any decision by Trump to allow Israel to use a bunker buster would have severe international consequences, not least of which potential nuclear contamination from the fallout of the attack. The sheer amount of technical logistics of even managing to transport the bunker buster to Israel without being spotted makes it a challenge to coordinate any such a strike with Israel. Like you can t really fly a 20-foot, 30,000 pound bomb through the skies without detection, ya know? There are also numerous US assets and personnel on the ground in the region that are actively in danger but would most certainly be attacked by Iran if the US got involved with the conflict. And those attacks could very well not be contained to just the Middle East region. This is all on top of the fact that Trump explicitly has condemned the war in Iraq started by President Bush and is aware of the strong MAGA faction that is against getting entangled in foreign wars. He ran on that isolationist, anti-war stance and was elected by a large faction of people who want to see the US pull out of all foreign wars and avoid future entanglements. While there are hawkish Republicans that are concerned about Iran s nuclear capabilities and are calling for involvement in the conflict, there is a HUGE swath of lawmakers and MAGA supporters that are opposed to further conflict, especially in the Middle East, after 20 years of war there. And this is a rare instance where a bipartisan coalition has formed. Republican Representative from Kentucky Thomas Massie has joined with Democrat Representative from California Ro Khanna to introduce a bipartisan war powers resolution that would prohibit involvement in the conflict with Iran without consent from Congress. And Democratic Senator Tim Kaine filed a similar resolution in the Senate. At this point it s hard to tell which faction of the MAGA movement will win out. Isolationism is a key component of the Project 2025 playbook, which calls for quote a comprehensive cost-benefit analysis of US participation in all international organizations and transforming NATO so that members are self-sufficient and less reliant on the US. And Trump has repeatedly called for an end to entanglement in foreign wars. That being said, the US has strong ties to Israel and our Congress and President have a long history of blindly supporting Israel even in the face of genocide and other atrocities. And another key tenet, THE key tenet, frankly, of the Project 2025 playbook is a concentration of power in the hands of the President by any means necessary. The constitution gives Congress the right to declare war, but will the orchestrators of Project 2025 take kindly to Congress asserting that power and constraining Trump? Maybe yes in this instance where it serves their isolationist ends. At the same time, there is a LOT of money to be made off war, so you better bet there are lobbyists in the ears of Trump and all our politicians promoting an escalation of the conflict. Here s my best guess the increased US military presence in the Middle East is posturing meant to intimidate Iran, but there are no real plans to get involved beyond providing defensive protection to Israel. This keeps Israel happy, benefits the lobbyists salivating at the idea of profiting off further war because it takes a lot of money and resources to deploy these military assets, while also avoiding further escalation that could put US bases and personnel at risk. This is a tenuous balancing act, though, and things could escalate at any minute should Iran strike US assets first. Especially given the damage to Iran s nuclear infrastructure over the last week and the US and Israel s own nuclear powers, the threat of Mutually Assured Destruction continues to be a strong motivator to avoid escalating the conflict and it s unlikely that Iran could withstand an actual war with the US, so despite increased hostilities I don t think either side wants the US to be involved. And given all this background, no I don t think World War 3 is imminent. Israel is not a member of NATO, Europe is not in a place to step in to support Israel nor is it under any obligation to do so, and the US getting involved in the conflict would create so much potential for escalation with very little gain on the part of the US. That being said, Turkey is a member of NATO and shares a border with Iran. It is an ally of Israel but also deeply critical of the genocide in Gaza. And Saudi Arabia is a close US ally and holds huge oil reserves and has worked towards normalizing relations with Israel but backed off over Israel s genocide in Gaza, which Saudi Arabia condemns. So I ll be keeping an eye on Turkey and Saudi Arabia, because I think it would take increased attacks across the Middle East, especially into NATO member Turkey before any sort of escalation of conflict to a global scale where Europe would also get involved such that this could be classified as a World War. If you want to support my work, all my videos are completely ad free and uncensored over on Patreon, Patreon dot com slash leeja miller. Thank you to my multi-platinum patrons Philly Tech Dude, Marc, Sarah Shelby, Travis, Art, David, R_H, L etranger (Lukus), Thomas Johnson, and Tay. Your generosity makes this channel what it is, so thank you! And if you liked this episode, you ll like the one from Monday about the politically motivated murders in Minnesota.

**Structure:** Opens by directly addressing a listener call-for-questions format (quoting two viewer questions by username about whether this is 'the whole WW3 thing'), establishing the video as an explainer answering audience confusion, then works through the Israel-Iran strikes chronologically, closing with a direct answer to the WWIII framing, a sponsor read (Ground News), and the standard Patreon/cross-promo sign-off.
**Framing:** Audience-Q&A framing: rather than opening with her own thesis, she opens by quoting named viewers' actual confused questions, positioning the whole video as a direct response to audience anxiety rather than a top-down analysis.

### 17. Why Are US Police So Bad? (2023-07-21) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xlvar36Ints)
**Metrics:** 576,027 views, 34,391 likes
**Thumbnail description:** Black-and-white archival photo of a long line of uniformed police officers each pointing a handgun directly at the camera, each face crossed out with a red horizontal bar over the eyes; Leeja Miller's cutout portrait (glasses, gold jewelry, alarmed expression) on the left; bold text reads 'ABOLISH THE POLICE?' in red and white on a black banner.
**Opening hook (verbatim, from transcript):**
> ﻿On the evening of May 25th, 2020, officers responded to a call on 38th and Chicago in South Minneapolis of a man reportedly using a forged 20 dollar bill to purchase cigarettes.

**Full transcript (verbatim, from YouTube's creator-provided English captions):**
> ﻿On the evening of May 25th, 2020, officers responded to a call on 38th and Chicago in South Minneapolis of a man reportedly using a forged 20 dollar bill to purchase cigarettes. The man, George Floyd, was sitting in a car with his friend when officers arrived, pulled out their g*ns, and ordered him out of his vehicle. After handcuffing the man, the officers attempted to put him into their squad car. Floyd resisted, saying he was claustrophobic. During the altercation, a number of other officers including Officer Chauvin arrived at the scene. Chauvin pulled Mr. Floyd away from the car at one point, causing him to fall to the ground, where he lay face down, still restrained by handcuffs, apparently in a distressed state. Witnesses began filming as a crowd formed around the altercation. The officers further restrained Mr. Floyd as Chauvin placed his knee on Mr. Floyd’s neck. What followed was 9 minutes of footage that would lead to George Floyd’s d**th and weeks of collective uprising against police violence across the United States and throughout the world. A final straw amid global pandemic tensions and after centuries of oppression and m*rder at the hands of racist police forces. Last month, after a 2 year investigation, the US Justice Department’s Civil Rights Division dropped a scathing 89 page report on the Minneapolis Police Department and the City of Minneapolis, finding that what happened to George Floyd was part of widespread, systemic problems in the MPD, a truth that residents here in Minneapolis already knew too well. And while corrupt, violent police are not unique to the United States, it is unique amongst similarly situated Western countries. So I set out to figure out why. What is it about our history, our culture, and our police that set us apart in all measurable ways from other similar countries? The answer is messy and layered, but one thing is clear: we have a serious problem and serious solutions need to be pushed at every level of government from your local city council all the way to Congress. THIS is why US police are so corrupt. Roll the intro. The DOJ report released last month found that the Minneapolis Police Department uses excessive force, including unjustified deadly force, unlawfully discriminates against Black and Native American people, violates the rights of people engaged in protected speech, and both MPD and the City, led by the absolutely cringe-worthy, incompetent Mayor Jacob Frey, discriminate against people with behavioral health disabilities when responding to calls for assistance. The report also found “persistent deficiencies in MPD’s accountability systems, training, supervision, and officer wellness programs, which contribute to violations of the Constitution and federal law.” And while the report was certainly damning, it merely confirmed what residents here, especially black residents, have known for their entire lives. And it is by no means unique to Minneapolis. In the United States, we have the lowest police training requirements of any similar country BY FAR. US police officers are more heavily armed than most other countries, as are the people they police. In Iceland, Ireland, New Zealand, Norway, and the UK, police are largely unarmed. In Iceland, where g*n ownership is common, the country has recorded one police involved shooting d**th in its history. In Ireland, most police aren’t even trained to use firearms. In the UK, police have themselves resisted calls for them to bear arms, saying it goes against their philosophy of policing by consent, meaning that their legitimacy is contingent on public approval of their actions, as opposed to brute force. What a concept. The rate of civilians k*lled by police annually in the US is more than three times higher than Canada, and four times higher than Australia. And yes that’s controlling for population size. And of course as we saw in the recent police k*lling and subsequent riots in France, violence especially against minorities at the hands of police is not unique to the United States. But it’s the RATE of that violence in the US that is staggering. Black people are dramatically overrepresented in the number of police k*llings in this country. While they make up only 12% of the US population, they represent over a quarter of all k*llings by police, often while being unarmed, unthreatening, and actively experiencing a mental health crisis. And while current policing standards and tactics are to blame, to get at the root of how we got here, we have to look at our history. In the early American colonies, policing was used as a means of securing private property and businesses from thieves and bandits, usually fuelled by fear of Native Americans coming and encroaching on their settlements in the night. So night watchmen and patrols were organized on a local level, and sometimes night watch was doled out as punishment for misdeeds. In the north, night watchmen were mainly concerned with enforcing puritanical rules against gambling and sex work. Though, because night watch duty was often given as punishment, it often led the night watchmen to engage in the vices they were meant to be policing. Sound familiar? As the south expanded and the slave trade was brought to the colonies, patrolmen and night watchmen were charged with policing enslaved Africans. The “slave patrol” and “slave catchers” were the first form of organized police in the south, originating in the colony of Carolina. They were the first professional “law enforcers” tasked with enforcing the slave codes, stopping any black person they saw, and chasing runaways. The idea of a centralized municipal police force originated in the UK around the turn of the 19th century. In rapidly urbanizing areas, moral reform, enforcement of the laws, and discipline of the new class of industrial laborers was thought to require a strong central policing body to maintain social order. And it wasn’t long before that idea spread to the United States. The Boston municipal police force was started in 1838 in response to influxes of immigrants from Germany and Ireland–immigrants who were seen as dangerous “others” that required additional surveillance and control. And of course it wasn’t long after then that the police forces started experiencing widespread corruption. By the 1850s, US police forces were prone to the gambling, booze, and sex workers that they were meant to be policing, and police captains and sergeants were appointed by politicians and used to harass their political opponents and to harass voters into voting certain ways. Then came the Civil War and the passage of the 13th Amendment in 1865 which provided that “Neither slavery nor involuntary servitude, except as a punishment for crime whereof the party shall have been duly convicted, shall exist within the United States.” The reconstruction era began in the South, along with the passage of the “black codes” which controlled where formerly enslaved people could go, work, live, travel, and how much they could be paid, essentially criminalizing all forms of black freedom. And because the 13th Amendment explicitly allowed for the enslavement and involuntary servitude of convicted criminals, it meant that the newly freed population of formerly enslaved people were at the mercy of a corrupt police force that knew that, once black people were found to be criminals, they would be “put back in their place.” And thanks to the black codes, that pattern of mass incarceration of black people started swiftly. The codes included very broad vagrancy laws that criminalized unemployed black people. And any black person who challenged the terms of their work by negotiating for higher salary, walking away from an abusive employer, or turning down a bad offer, would then be criminalized for vagrancy because they were unemployed and therefore no longer protected by the 13th Amendment while incarcerated. And under the convict leasing system, prisons would lease their detainees to private companies who would earn huge profits off the backs of incarcerated black people working in mining, railroads, and other industries. Police, as arbiters and enforcers of the law, served as the ultimate reminder to black people of their lower status in society, and it ensured that wealthy, powerful people could continue to extract labor from black bodies. And people knew their police forces were corrupt, especially during prohibition when many cops fell in with organized crime rings that proliferated during this period, so in 1929 President Hoover appointed the 11-member Wickersham Commission to examine the corruption in America’s law enforcement. Over the course of two years, they produced volumes of reports documenting widespread corruption. The resulting reforms included a remapping of police precincts to reduce the chance of political influence, increased recruitment standards, and diversification of police officers from different communities. By the 1950s, reforms in the police force prioritized quote unquote professionalism. Efforts to consolidate and centralize police forces were intended to help maintain order and prevent corruption. The goal was to create a sense of police omnipresence so criminals never felt outside the watchful eye of THE LAW. Policing became a full blown career path, and centralization and the quasi-militaristic ranking within the system caused departments to become more disconnected from the public. And while these reforms curbed some of the flagrant corruption in earlier police forces, it did nothing to root out the inherent racism that had existed as the foundational pretext for the existence of the police departments to begin with. And this reality was put on stark display in the 1960s during the civil rights movement, when the new nightly news and newspapers published disturbing photographs of police brutality during street protests, with many cops resorting to horseback, whips, and dogs as a means of controlling the masses, tactics that harkened back to the original slave chasers from whence the police departments came. In retaliation, Lindon B Johnson announced his “War on Crime” through the passage of the Crime Control and Safe Streets Act of 1968, which created grants for local police to purchase military grade weapons to suppress riots. This change sent an increasingly militarized police force into largely impoverished black neighborhoods, increasing surveillance and criminalization of those communities. The practice called “Stop and Frisk” was all but codified into law in 1968 through the Supreme Court case Terry v. Ohio which gave police officers legal authority to stop and search someone if they have “reasonable suspicion” that the person has committed, is committing, or is about to commit a crime. Stop and frisk was used for decades to disproportionately target young black men and wasn’t found to be an unconstitutional infringement against the 4th Amendment’s protection against searches and seizures until 2013. After LBJ’s “war on crime”, Nixon’s war on drugs further criminalized the black community by focusing heavily on the policing of heroin which was running rampant in black communities at the time. And despite the fact that throughout history the police have been used to quell and prevent labor organizing, by the 70s and 80s, police unions became a battleground, leading to widespread “blue flus” a tactic the police would use to walk out on the job in retaliation for budget cuts or any other perceived wrongdoing. And while today nationwide union membership has dropped from its high in the 80s, higher membership rates among police unions today mean they have strong bargaining power to resist reform and extensive resources to influence campaigns and initiate litigation to block reform. For example, a single New York City police union has spent over $1 million dollars on state and local races in the last decade. And at least one study has shown that after officers gain access to collective bargaining rights there is a substantial increase in k*llings of civilians. So where does that leave us today? Today, state and local governments spend about 115 BILLION dollars on police every year, most of it coming from taxpayer money, plus a $54 million dollar chunk coming from federal funds distributed through grant programs. State to state and even county to county, training for police officers varies widely. In many instances, the hours of training required to become a g*n slinging police officer hover around 650, while becoming a cosmetologist takes 3,000 hours, a plumber 3,500, and a lawyer on average 4,725 hours. And that last one, the amount of training it takes to be a lawyer, is important. To write the laws and interpret the laws for clients comes with a bachelor’s degree, plus 3 additional years of schooling, multiple internships, and a rigorous bar exam, not to mention continuing education requirements and ethical standards that must be maintained. Cops are tasked with enforcing the laws that lawyers and legislators write, which requires often split second interpretation of how the law applies to the specific situation they are responding to, all while holding a loaded g*n, and on average they have about 16 weeks of training with nothing more than a high school diploma as a prerequisite and no national board of ethics or oversight unless you count the DOJ coming in after tragedy to do an investigation. MAKE IT MAKE SENSE! In Europe similar training programs require more than 3 years to complete. And as I said, the manner and type of training that police receive varies dramatically throughout the country, but on average they are receiving 213 hours of operations training, which I assume means how to fill out and file the right paperwork after incidents, followed by 168 hours of firearms and self defense and use of force tactics, then 89 hours of self improvement, unclear what that means, then 86 hours, or a little over 2 40-hour weeks on legal education. TWO WEEKS. Followed by a grand total of 13 hours of domestic violence training and 10 HOURS of mental illness training. This despite the fact that most police k*llings start in response to non-violent offenses, cases where no crime was reported, and situations in which the victim was having a mental health crisis. Approximately one in four people shot and k*lled by police between 2015 and 2020 showed signs of mental illness. And there are mixed reports on whether the police are even effective at curbing crime, especially in cities with large black populations. In those cities, research has shown that increasing police presence does nothing to curb violent crime and instead increases the rate of arrest for petty, non-violent crime like loitering and disorderly conduct, unnecessarily siphoning black people, especially black youth, into the criminal justice system. A recent study of the California police force found that LA County sheriff’s officers spent 88% of their time on officer-initiated stops, and 79% of that time was spent on traffic violations. About 3 out of every 4 hours that Sacramento sheriff’s officers spent investigating traffic violations were for stops that ended in warnings or no action. More of the department’s budget was going towards fruitless traffic stops than in response to 911 calls, basically wasting millions of public dollars, especially considering that black people were more than twice as likely than white residents to be stopped by sheriff's deputies in some counties. So we have an undertrained, heavily militarized, ineffective police force that has been found, time and again, to abuse its power especially towards people of color, especially towards black people. But we also have a country where its citizens are armed to the FUCKING TEETH, with very little by way of public assistance for mental health and addiction services, where most of its population is one $500 emergency away from homelessness, where a large portion of its population is actively homeless, where there is no safety net, and where institutionalized racism has left entire groups of people disenfranchized and economically bereft, all conditions that lead to increased levels of crime and incarceration in a community that is already overly policed and overly incarcerated. It’s a fuckin mess. So where do we go from here?? Well, as I’ve already said, the police unions are often fighting tooth and nail against reforms or budget cuts, but there has been some progress towards some semblance of reforms in the wake of George Floyd’s d**th. The MPD has agreed to move forward with a consent decree as a result of this DOJ investigation. After George Floyd was m*rdered, MPD policy now prohibits neck restraints, and officers can’t use certain crowd control weapons without approval of the MPD chief. After the MPD k*lled a man named Amir Locke during a no-knock warrant execution, MPD has banned the use of no-knock warrants. A no-knock warrant was what led to Brionna Taylor’s d**th as well. Minneapolis has also launched a promising mental health response program where 911 dispatchers can send qualified mental health professionals to handle a non-threatening mental health crisis, as opposed to sending armed police officers with 10 HOURS OF MENTAL HEALTH TRAINING. Anecdotally I have heard positive stories about their response in scenarios where a cop would have unnecessarily escalated the situation. But despite these reforms, unnecessary k*llings by cops have continued in Minneapolis, our black population continues to be incredibly segregated and over-policed, and the Minneapolis police department has been severely understaffed since George Floyd’s k*lling, because residents fucking hate the cops, and for good reason, and the cops are undertrained and have no clue how to handle the situation. In 2021, 56% of voters in Minneapolis rejected a ballot question that would have removed the MPD from the city charter and replaced it with a public-health oriented Department of Public Safety, a move that would have signaled a sea change and brought about much needed overhauls of a clearly broken policing system. And our mayor Jacob Frey is a useless Democrat who was actively booed off stage for his response during the riots after George Floyd’s m*rder, who likes to show up and virtue signal at juneteenth celebrations, literally what is happening here, while simultaneously begging the state for more police funding and lying to his constituents. And black and brown people keep dying at the hands of cops. As Mother Jones put it after the no-knock k*lling of Amir Locke, “Minneapolis became globally infamous for anti-Black racism and police m*rder not because of just one policy, but because Derek Chauvin—who trained Minneapolis cops for a living—felt it within his right to choke the life out of George Floyd on the corner of a busy intersection in front of a crowd of people in broad daylight over a twenty dollar bill. Because cops believe they can do that, here and elsewhere.”[a] And something needs to change. But people are scared out of their minds, which has led to things like that referendum being voted down, and a whole lot of inaction. Because people, white people specifically, can’t imagine a world without police to protect them. Because we are the only ones who feel protected by them. And since the m*rder of George Floyd, many of us, myself included, have come to realize the blind spot that that privilege has afforded us. That I got to grow up feeling safe when I saw a cop car nearby. Getting pulled over and not having to fear for my life. But for people who haven’t realized that the cops are only protecting a very pale portion of us, the idea of “abolishing the police” seems absolutely insane. Imagine the chaos. IMAGINE THE CRIME!! But of course, for most people calling for the abolition of the police, it’s not a call for anarchy to reign, for laws to be done away with and for all of us to live in a state of perpetual self defense against the masses. It’s a call for a new form of law enforcement, one that is made by and for the community, to serve the community instead of terrorize it, to meet the community’s actual needs, for mental health intervention, for assistance during homelessness. Because when you have an institution with centuries of violent precedent against minorities, that was founded on the basis of controlling those minorities, maybe it's better to create a new institution altogether, instead of working within the confines of an institution that has proven itself, time and again, to be impervious to reform. The problem, however, with reforming the police, or even with abolishing the police in favor of a community centered public safety task force, is that we also have to consider the larger picture of the society we have built for ourselves. The police and their brutality don’t exist in a bubble. They exist in a country with deep racial divides, where half of the populace is fear mongered by Fox News to believe that black people and immigrants are the enemy. A country where there are approximately 120 g*ns per every 100 people. Where any semblance of a public safety net has been systematically stripped away, so most people are a single $500 emergency away from homelessness. Where people are struggling with addiction and mental health emergencies, largely caused by the society they live in, where people have to work 3 jobs to make ends meet because minimum wage hasn’t increased in decades. Where entire generations have no hope of ever owning their own home or having any sort of support in their old age. Where having a child is forced upon you but you are given zero resources once you bring that child into the world. This is an incredibly sick, addicted, unstable, and isolating society we’ve built for ourselves. It’s every one for themself. And then we take some of those people, often some of the least educated among us, and we give them g*ns and military grade weapons and we say alright go enforce the law. Of course that’s not going to work. So along with defunding the police, we also need funding for the basic human necessities that are not being met by our current societal configuration. That’s jobs, jobs that actually pay a living wage. Or better yet, a universal basic income so that your survival isn’t dependent upon how many hours you’re able to labor. Mental health services. Support for parents and children. Access to abortion for people who aren’t ready to be parents. Stricter g*n control laws so that people aren’t wandering the streets with AR15s. Decriminalization of drugs. See Portugal for a promising example of how drug decriminalization can be done effectively. Access to addiction care instead of stigmatization. And access to affordable housing for the unhoused. So instead of writing off the idea of police abolition, I would challenge you to consider whether the current configuration is even working. Or who it’s working for and why. And whether you might also want to live in a society that does better. Cuz I sure as shit do. I’ve included some helpful links and resources below if you’re still struggling with wrapping your head around the idea of police abolition and you want more information. If you liked this video you might also like my video on why Americans are so obsessed with g*ns. Thank you to my Patreon supporters, including my newest patrons, and an extra special shout out to my multip-platinum patron Brett Piontek. If you’re interested in behind the scenes content, access to my research and show notes, content about my dog, and all sorts of other stuff, consider joining me over on Patreon today. Thanks have a good day BYE.

**Structure:** Opens with a minute-by-minute narrative recounting of the George Floyd arrest and killing, then moves into the video's real subject, the history and structural design of American policing, with no sponsor read anywhere in the video (a rarity among these 20), closing with a call to learn more about police abolition and the standard Patreon/cross-promo sign-off.
**Framing:** True-crime-style narrative cold open used to ground an otherwise structural/historical argument; the single named, dated incident (Floyd, May 25 2020) stands in for the systemic claim the rest of the video makes, and the absence of a sponsor read on this topic appears to be a deliberate choice.

### 18. Are We Allowed To Laugh When A CEO Dies? (2024-12-09) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YCpVncEXZVE)
**Metrics:** 570,838 views, 36,112 likes
**Thumbnail description:** Split composite: a smiling man in a quarter-zip sweater on the left, a blurred grainy still of a hooded figure in the center pulled from surveillance footage, and Leeja Miller's cutout portrait (glasses, gold jewelry, startled expression) on the right; bold white text on a black banner reads 'AMERICANS AGREE ON SOMETHING??'.

**Structure:** Opens with the news hook (public jokes/memes about UnitedHealthcare CEO Brian Thompson's killing being condemned as insensitive) and an immediate dark-humor line of her own, then a sponsor read (Ground News, comparing media bias on the coverage), then an argument about why the public reaction is a symptom of healthcare-system anger rather than a moral failure, closing with the standard Patreon/cross-promo sign-off.
**Framing:** Provocative-question framing stated directly in the title and thumbnail; opens by immediately validating the discomfort ('at least...he died doing what he loved') before turning it into an argument about systemic anger, rather than building up to the provocation gradually.

### 19. The Manosphere to Alt-Right Pipeline (we should all be talking about this) (2023-09-27) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U-V_xf2gAiI)
**Metrics:** 567,769 views, 32,932 likes

**Opening hook (verbatim, from transcript):**
