**Full transcript (verbatim, from YouTube's creator-provided English captions):**
> ﻿“The fact is, when you give women rights, they destroy absolutely everything around them, no matter what other variable is involved... Even if you become the ultimate alpha male, some stupid bitch will still ruin your life.” — Andrew Anglin, Neo-Nazi “The vagina is the perfect representation of the nature of females. An empty vessel, a hole, a void with no identity of its own.” – Matt Forney, alt-right blogger “I’m in a state of exuberance that we have a President who rates women on a 1-10 scale in the same way that we do and evaluates women by their appearance and feminine attitude. We may have to institute a new feature called “Would Trump bang?’ to signify the importance of feminine beauty ideals….” Roosh V, Alt-Right blogger/PUA “Trump Embodies Red Pill thinking into every fiber of his being. It’s important that we support him” - Anonymous Men’s Rights Redditor “Trump might be one of our last hopes to overthrow the current system and banish the liar forever. More importantly he’s the embodiment of everything masculine.Trump becoming president in the United States means the West will eventually reflect him. We are taking our manhood back. No more slaves. Freedom For all” - Anonymous Men’s Rights Redditor Since the dawn of the internet, it has been a place for people from all fringes of society to come together and find community in their shared beliefs. When those shared beliefs are violent and create a tribal-like focus on identity and power, those online communities grow like wildfire and eventually escape the confines of the digital world and create real consequences that can end in violence, death, and even insurrection. A lot of energy has been spent focusing on the religious radicalization of kids on the internet and the danger they pose to national security, but not nearly enough attention has been paid to the radicalization of white men on the internet, and the danger that poses not only to girls and women but to democracy writ large. Because once a person becomes radicalized around one set of beliefs, such as that women are the enemy and men are the real victims of feminism, it becomes a lot easier to jump to the belief that immigrants and women are the enemy, and white men are the real victims of feminism, globalism, and the cultural Marxism that has taken hold of every corner of life in the United States. It’s a slippery slope that leads to killing sprees, violence, and wholesale insurrection. This is the manosphere to alt-right pipeline we should all be talking about. Roll the intro. As a person who exists publicly, especially a woman who shares her opinions online, I am hyper aware of safety and security. And it's easier than ever to find personal information about people online. This is due in part to Data brokers, which are corporations that collect your personal information from a variety of sources like government public records and social media, and then aggregate that information and sell it to other companies. That's where those scammy looking websites that allow you to search for people or do background checks on people get all that information. Legally, there are very few online privacy laws in the United States, so the data you might consider to be private may not be private at all. That information can then be used to steal your identity or dox you, where someone posts your contact information online to encourage people to harass you. Not only that, but they also collect the information of your family members, often posting it all together so it's so much easier to connect other people to you and expose all of your information online. All this data hanging out on the internet can have actual consequences in the real world. I personally use DeleteMe to help keep my information off the internet and private, which gives me the peace of mind I need to come on here week after week and share my opinions publicly online. Once you sign up for DeleteMe, they send you a welcome email and you can get started right away. Tell them exactly what information you want deleted, and their privacy experts take it from there. It's super easy and absolutely worthwhile for the peace of mind it provides. You can sign up for DeleteMe and get that peace of mind for yourself at the link in the description, and use code LEEJA for 20% off your plan. Thanks DeleteMe! The manosphere is the name for the place online where multiple different men’s groups congregate, generally in forums and websites like reddit, 4chan, and 8chan. These groups are many, disparate, and disorganized but they include Men's Rights Activists, Incels, and Pick Up Artists. We’ll get into the nitty gritty differences between each, but suffice it to say they are communities where men are able to gather, voice their concerns and opinions, get advice, and connect with other like minded people. While many sources point to the internet as increasing the feelings of loneliness and isolation the world over, it has also allowed for isolated communities and individuals to gain a sense of belonging, friendship, and camaraderie when they’ve struggled to find it in the “real world.” And one of the most isolated groups of people in the world are men. Especially men who feel that, for whatever reason, they don’t fit in with regular people or who struggle with friendships and feelings of isolation. And a lot of men struggle with isolation. A 2021 survey found that only 21% of men said they had received emotional support from a friend within the last week, compared to 41% of women. And just 25% of men said they had said ‘I love you’ to a friend recently, as opposed to 49% of women. Between 1995 and 2021, the number of men reporting that they had no close friends jumped from 3 to 15 percent. NO close friends. And the number of men saying they had at least six close friends went from 55% to 27%. And that isolation has real consequences. Social isolation can significantly increase your risk of premature death from all causes, a risk that could be just as high as smoking and physical inactivity. It has also been associated with a 50% increased risk of dementia. And along with that loneliness comes the risk of self harm. Between 2000 and 2020, 800,000 people died by suicide. Men made up about 80% of them. And the pandemic only made it worse. In 2022, the CDC recorded nearly 50,000 such deaths, the highest number ever recorded. There are many factors that may explain the reason for this isolation. Again, the pandemic didn’t help but male isolation has been a trend for decades. Looking at that isolation through a feminist lens, one piece of the puzzle is that those feelings of isolation stem from the patriarchy, which defines masculinity in stark opposition to femininity, and requires that in order to be “real men” men must be strong, emotionally “controlled”, confident, they must be able to provide for their families, they must be traditionally handsome and muscular, and they must show no sign of weakness. The absence of any of these traits makes a man vulnerable to attacks on his masculinity. The consequence of this is generations of men who have no clue how to connect with other people because real connection requires vulnerability, something they’re taught never to show. This leads to increased incidences of suicide and violence. But the anonymity of the internet provides an outlet for men who feel they don’t fit the mold. They turn to the internet for advice and camaraderie. This can lead men, especially young men who are at their most insecure as they’re trying to figure out their identities in all of this, to turn to forums to seek out advice from others for how to better fit this mold. This can start as something as innocuous as seeking out advice for how to talk to girls, and maybe what workouts to do to build some muscle and improve their fitness. They may find helpful forum posts from men who felt very similarly to them and have managed to change their lifestyle a bit and benefited from it. They feel seen, and they feel a sense of connection they’ve been searching for. So they dig deeper into the forum. Sprinkled in with the advice for high intensity workouts and protein supplements they also see posts that suggest perhaps part of the problem isn’t just that they need to change themselves but, actually, that women are causing a lot of this pain and isolation through their rejection of them, so maybe women are the problem. And this is a tempting explanation, especially to explain the anecdotal experiences of lonely men. “Hey that girl Sarah I had a crush on in the 8th grade told me I was too short and everyone laughed at me. It’s her fault that I feel this way. And I see a few similar anecdotes on this forum, so it must be women generally who are the enemy.” It can make a person feel seen and like maybe they’re not the complete piece of shit they feel they are. That in fact they’re fine it’s OTHER people who are the problem. This is where the essential disconnect happens. Instead of having someone come in and tell them the “other” people who are the problem is society writ large and the predominance of the patriarchy which requires strict adherence to gender roles at the risk of social isolation, which harms both women and men, instead someone is there telling them no no you aren’t the problem, and the other people who are the problem are women. You are in fact a victim of women. And it’s not women who are reinforcing the patriarchy that are the problem, it’s actually women who are feminists. They’ve been given too much power. In fact things were better in the 50s when they had less power. Men back then definitely didn’t feel isolated or bad about themselves, because they took their rightful place as the patriarch of the family, women didn’t fight their places in society, and everything was right with the world. It’s feminism to blame for all of this and the women who adhere to it. And now you have a young man who’s gone from casually consuming tips about protein powder and how to talk to girls to a young man who believes he’s the victim of a vast network of tyrannical women whose empowerment is keeping men from taking their true place in society. And the deeper this young man gets, the more he personally and emotionally identifies with this online community, because it’s the first time he’s found an explanation for his pain that both makes sense and also takes his personal responsibility out of the equation. I’m not the problem, I’m the hapless victim. And then, what the man DOES about this feeling depends on which flavor of manosphere our hypothetical young man has found first or most identifies with. There’s the Men's Rights Activists or MRAs, who have been active since long before the invention of the internet and tend to focus on how society is rigged against men, especially family law courts where fathers’ rights are being infringed, and employment law where women are getting ahead by playing the woman card. Then there’s the more Andrew Tate flavored Pick Up Artists or PUAs, who have also been around longer than the internet, who teach young men how to talk to girls, not from a place of genuine interest or respect but from a place of psychological manipulation. They see this as a sexual war which men have to win by any means necessary, including coercion, gaslighting, and even rape, with leaders in the community advocating for the legalization of date rape and teaching it as a skill in their books, seminars, and many online forum posts. Similarly, Incels, short for involuntary celibate, blame their forced celibacy on women who they see as trying to control them by holding out on sex, something that these men across the board believe should be available to them at all times, and feminism is to blame for women not being as sexually available to them as they were say back in the 1950s when everything was perfect. While spending their days obsessing over women: how to get them, how to have sex with them, how to hurt them, how to control them, and how they’re ruining their lives, these men also openly despise women, posting some of the most vile dehumanizing violent tirades against women that can be found on the internet. These communities are many and varied and not neatly organized, but they generally tend to have common throughlines of violent attitudes towards women, who they believe are undeserving of respect, as well as generalized feelings of victimhood. They are victims of a system that has been rigged against them in favor of feminism by tyrannical women who want to see them suffer and women generally must be punished for it. A recent Article from the Atlantic explains further, “[A]nti-feminism offers to address a genuine sense of grievance. Sites such as 4chan, another anonymous message board, have developed a whole language to talk about the hurt young men feel: “Staceys” (conventionally attractive, sexually available white women) date “Chads” (jocks), ignoring “nice guys” who get “friendzoned.” Men who claim to be feminists are “manginas” and “white knights.” If these men date feminists, they are probably being “cucked” (cheated on), because women like real men, not “beta males.” What starts as Why won’t girls sleep with me?—an appeal to a wounded sense of entitlement—becomes Why can’t women be controlled?” And this appeal to male entitlement and feelings of inadequacy, the threat to their masculinity caused by lack of stable well-paying jobs, women who are able to work and pay their way without the help of men, more women who don’t want to date men at all, declining birth rates, and a whole host of other phenomena that challenge the status quo and paint the world as scary and risky, can lead to further radicalization. And so one thing leads to another and these men go from anonymous online forum commenters to very public very violent offenders very quickly. Take, for example, the 2014 mass shooting in Isla Vista, California that ended in 22 injuries and 6 deaths before the shooter took his own life. In the hours leading up to the shooting, he had posted a 141 page diatribe in which he explained his hatred of women, fuelled largely by an intense frustration over his virginity. In the document he described himself as “The closest thing there is to a living God.” Saying he was the “ideal magnificent gentleman” and could not comprehend why women wouldn’t want to have sex with him, so his only choice was to “exact revenge on the society” that had denied him sex and love, declaring at the end “I am the true victim in all of this. I am the good guy.” His attacks centered on a sorority house at a local college. The shooter has since become a martyr in incel online communities. His 141 page document has since been adopted in various corners of the manosphere, with excerpts used to justify further vitriol. 4 years after the Isla Vista shooting, a man posted a Facebook tribute to the shooter before driving his truck through a crowd in Toronto, injuring 15 people and killing 11. In light of these and numerous other attacks perpetrated by members of these online communities, in 2018 the Southern Poverty Law Center, which tracks extremist groups, for the first time classified male supremacy as a distinct ideology of hate, noting that, “like white supremacy, male supremacy is driven by fear and anger at the loss of white male status.” The Anti-Defamation League’s report on the connection between misogyny and radicalization paints a bleak picture: “The underlying theme on incel message boards (readily found on 4Chan and Reddit) is that the current sexual “marketplace” gives women too much freedom to choose their partners, which leaves some men being cheated out of their sexual birthright. Some incels believe that by denying them sex, women are committing “reverse rape” – which they argue is just as damaging and harmful as actual rape, and should be included in the #MeToo conversation.” Indeed the Me Too movement itself likely only exacerbated these feelings that men and man’s rightful place at the top is being threatened at all sides. The irony in all of this of course is that these men who are experiencing social isolation because of the patriarchy–their feelings of inadequacy for not living up to the male ideal required by the patriarchy and their inability to connect with others because vulnerability isn’t an option–have led them to hate feminists, the very people who are trying to end the patriarchy and who often critique the gendered requirements for men because they hurt everyone, including men. And while hating women, they also share tips on how to confidently talk to women and how to lose weight and get muscular, in short, how to better fit into the patriarchy which is the source of their despair. And as men are drawn deeper into the dismal recesses of the manosphere, the message transforms from misogyny to misogyny with racial overtones. As one researcher put it, “Once you engage with the idea that a social-justice-warrior club and the feminist movement have increased the precarity of men, that moves over time into the increased precarity and endangerment of ‘the West.’ And this slow change from manosphere as a personal philosophy to manosphere as a political ideology was documented by a research team who looked at hundreds of comments over years of posts on the Red Pill subreddit. The Red Pill refers to the movie The Matrix in which the main character must choose between two pills, the blue pill and the red pill. The blue pill allows him to stay blind to the real way that society works, and the red pill allows him to see the true nature of society, something you can never unsee. The main character chooses the red pill. In the manosphere, the idea of being “red pilled” means you believe you finally see the world for what it is–a place where women have taken over, control men through sex, and men have become the hapless victims of feminism and modernity. Interestingly that term “red pilled” is also used by the alt-right in regard to their own flavor of world view, which we’ll get into. Researchers scoured the Red Pill subreddit and indexed the posts in order to analyze how it went from decidedly a-political (politics weren’t discussed and in fact were frowned upon, it was a space for men to air their grievances and politics had no place) to decidedly political (Trump is our man and it is our moral imperative to vote for him to save ourselves). It became less about the man as individual and more about men as a cohesive group that can affect political change. And this is directly tied to the election of Donald Trump. Trump said the things that these men felt they couldn’t say out loud, except in the manosphere where they post violent diatribes against women and sometimes go out and actually kill women. Trump made violent misogynists feel seen after years of feeling like they had to be underground and anonymous to voice some of the things Trump said out loud. And there’s nothing like making a group of people feel seen to get them to come over to your side. A 2021 study researched the migration patterns of commenters from manosphere posts to alt-right posts on both Reddit and YouTube. The study concluded “our results suggest that there is a large overlap between the user bases of the Alt-right and of the Manosphere and that members of the Manosphere have a bigger chance to engage with far right content” than the control group. Previous research has found that content creators on YouTube who share the same platform and often engage in public debate with each other, bringing their communities together and engaging directly with them, can create “radicalization pathways” on YouTube. And studies have shown that members of other online communities do frequently migrate to channels espousing more extreme views. The 2021 study found that the “overlap between users in the Manosphere and the Alt right is substantial and it is common for users who once commented exclusively in the Manosphere to eventually engage with Alt-right content.” And it makes sense, as I’ve said, that a person who has been radicalized to think “women are the enemy and I am the victim and women should be put in their place” would also be susceptible to further radicalization, indeed reports have shown that alt-right leaders do active recruitment in the manosphere because of this very premise, with misogyny the handy common denominator that serves as a useful outreach mechanism. This is in part because misogyny is one of the most acceptable parts of the alt-right world view. For example, while offensive it is still far less inflammatory to say that men are smarter than women than it is to say white people are smarter than black people. The first one could be openly said on Fox News with little blowback, the second is often implied on Fox news but never said out loud. So they can get their toe in the door by saying the misogynistic things casually and then slowly weaving in the things that can no longer be said because society generally really frowns on eugenics these days. And then further narratives can be woven in to help explain real world phenomena in a way that fits in a with a certain world view. Birth rates are falling. Women who reach higher education levels do tend to have fewer children. Men can no longer earn a wage that supports a family of four. Working women have independence and are no longer dependent on men. Women can control their fertility and bank accounts now, so they no longer have to be subservient to men and their lives are no longer dictated by family or the church. The provided explanations, that feminism has ruined society and women are fighting to oppress men at every level of society and government, can quickly turn into a full fledged conspiracy theory similar to the kind of anti-Semitic QAnon conspiracies that have flourished since Trump’s election. When you believe that women are systematically oppressing men and there’s a vast coverup to the point that you need to be Red Pilled to see it, you’re much more likely to believe there’s also a cabal of elites and jews running the world’s economy and praying on children. F. Roger Devlin is an example of where these two worlds intertwine, and then are given further legitimacy by people like him who are pseudo academics with published books and therefore have an air of authority that bigots and extremists can then point to to justify their ideology. F. Roger Devlin is a white nationalist who has argued that women’s liberation has hurt white men’s ability to procreate, because these women go off and get degrees and focus on their careers and are therefore less likely to get married and have children to perpetuate the white race. Therefore, feminism and self-actualized women threaten the existence of the white race which has upset the natural order of things. And the number of white supremacists who have been charged with or advocate in explicit favor for domestic violence is staggering. In 2017 the white supremacist group Vanguard America promoted their new “women’s division” saying “The woman has her own battlefield. With every child that she brings into the world, she fights her battle for the nation. Strong nations grow from strong families.” And in alt-right spaces, women who are friends with or date non-white men are considered race traitors who are damaged and generally useless. The common throughline in the manosphere and alt right spaces is the desire to control women, and the feeling of entitlement. That these men had a birthright that was taken from them: certain jobs, racial or socioeconomic status, sex, they deserve it all simply because they exist, because that is the natural order of things. And all these groups within the manosphere and the alt right have been emboldened by the Trump presidency–they are validated and empowered by seeing themselves represented in the White House. Sounds nice, I’ve never experienced that. While misogynists in the manosphere have been migrating to alt-right corners of the internet for a while, it turns out that Trump was the ultimate uniter of these two groups, and the ultimate harbinger that brought them from dark anonymous online communities and out onto the streets in public. We saw this at the Unite the Right rally in Charlottesville in 2017, where a group of mostly angry young white men felt emboldened enough to shout The Jews will Not Replace Us en mass in public. And we saw it on January 6th, when groups of mostly angry white men stormed the capitol building, feeling they had personally been attacked because the man who represented them, both politically but also personally, wasn’t given back the presidency. Numerous of the men in the crowd that day have been charged with domestic assault or intimate partner violence. Based on what we’ve discussed in this video, that a violent insurrectionist would also exhibit violence towards women should come as no surprise. There’s a lot of suggested band-aid solutions for this, such as greater enforcement of censorship in online spaces like Reddit or teaching law enforcement how to better handle online harassment and abuse before it becomes physical, but those don’t get at the underlying problem: men are not ok. Like I know it’s a fun meme to throw around: aRe MeN oKaY?? But like truly no they are not. Check on the men in your life. Based on the stats that YouTube shows me, I know that 75% of you watching are men. Are y’all ok??? Check in with each other. Tell your buds that you love them. Get a therapist and tell everyone that you got a therapist so that it becomes more normalized for men to get therapists and talk about therapy. Be vulnerable with the boys. Ask them how their hearts feel. Ask them what pain they feel in their souls. Ok no but seriously it’s really not hard to text a friend and say hey how are you doing, how do you feel about your job or your kids or your marriage or your life? What are you working towards? Be in community with each other. Reach out especially to the young men in your life. They’re at a point where they likely feel really insecure and unsure of themselves. Show them what it’s like to exist as a well adjust man who can speak about his feelings and doesn’t fucking hate women. See if you can become a mentor to someone in your community, either professionally or through your alma mater or something. Statistically 15% of you have no close friends and that’s not necessarily your fault, it’s really fucking hard to exist in this world I get it. Try taking classes or joining a group based around a hobby you have, or pick up a hobby you think might be interesting so that you have a reason to get out of the house and meet people. Yeah it’s gonna be awkward as fuck, all of this, the meeting new people, the small talk, the reaching out to your pal to see how his heart feels, but it’s not gonna kill ya. The loneliness might though. And if it doesn’t kill you, it might kill me or other women in your life. So maybe do it anyway? If you liked this video you might also like my video about why conservatives are obsessed with voter fraud. Thank you to my Patreon supporters, including my newest patrons, and an extra special shout out to my multi-platinum patrons Safiya Samms, Anthony Jiles, and Brett Piontek. If you’re interested in behind the scenes content, access to my research and show notes, content about my dog, and all sorts of other stuff, consider joining me over on Patreon today.

**Structure:** Opens with three stacked, named, sourced quotes from neo-Nazi and alt-right figures (Andrew Anglin, Matt Forney) about women, presented without commentary before she begins narrating, then a sponsor read (DeleteMe), then a structural argument tracing how online misogyny content pipelines young men toward the alt-right, closing with a call to intervene with the men in your life and the standard Patreon/cross-promo sign-off.
**Framing:** Quote-collage cold open used as a content warning and evidence bank simultaneously: letting the source material's own ugliest lines open the video, attributed by name, before she says a word herself.

### 20. How Watergate Ruined Everything (2023-07-05) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=i8dRp2oQL8U)
**Metrics:** 564,030 views, 24,712 likes
**Thumbnail description:** Black-and-white archival photo of the Watergate office complex circled in red with a red X and an arrow pointing to the break-in floor; overlaid court filing documents and a row of mugshot photos with eyes redacted by red bars sit in the lower left; Leeja Miller's cutout portrait (glasses, gold jewelry, shocked expression) on the right.
**Opening hook (verbatim, from transcript):**
> ﻿Just after midnight on Saturday, June 17, 1972, at the Watergate complex in Washington DC, security guard Frank Wills was conducting his usual nightly rounds. Being the thorough security guard that he was, he noticed something strange.

**Full transcript (verbatim, from YouTube's creator-provided English captions):**
> ﻿Just after midnight on Saturday, June 17, 1972, at the Watergate complex in Washington DC, security guard Frank Wills was conducting his usual nightly rounds. Being the thorough security guard that he was, he noticed something strange. Tape was stuck to the latches on the doors leading from the underground car park up to the offices of the democratic national committee. The tape allowed the doors to shut but remain unlatched and unlocked. Frank removed the tape, assuming it was placed there inadvertently. A short time later, Frank returned to the area and discovered that someone was in the building and had retaped the latches on the doors. Suspecting a break-in, Frank called the police. Across the street inside a hotel room with clear views of the Watergate complex and DNC headquarter offices, Alfred Baldwin had set down his binoculars, distracted by the film Attack of the Puppet People playing on his hotel TV. If he had been observing the building like he was supposed to be, he would have seen 3 plainclothes police officers pull up and head straight for the DNC sixth floor office suite. By the time he realized what was happening inside the building, it was too late. The police apprehended five men inside the DNC headquarters. They were later charged with attempted burglary and attempted interception of telephone and other communications. Within hours of the burglars’ arrests, the FBI discovered that two of the men had connections to E. Howard Hunt, a CIA operative who himself had connections to Nixon’s secret group known as the White House Plumbers, established to help stop security leaks and covertly investigate other sensitive security matters. What followed was a cover up, and then a cover up of the cover up, that would unravel the United States Presidency, and put the country’s system of checks and balances to the test in a way that was unprecedented in the history of the country. Nixon’s playbook of denial, deception, and disinformation was later put on steroids and applied throughout the Trump presidency in ways we are just now uncovering. Nixon walked so that Trump could run, and we’re still dealing with the fallout from Watergate to this day. This is how the Watergate scandal changed American politics forever. Roll the intro. Thank you to Factor for partnering with me on today’s video! Y’all it is SUMMERTIME and I am trying to have the fullest bestest hot girl summer a gal can have. And you know what that means??? I don’t have time to cook. I don’t want to stand in a hot kitchen. But I also don’t want to spend a ton of money on takeout all the time because I’m tryna be FISCALLY RESPONSIBLE. What’s a girl to do?? Factor. That’s what. Factor delivers fresh, never frozen meals directly to your doorstep, so you can skip the time prepping and cooking and cleaning in the hot kitchen, eat something truly delicious, and then get back to enjoying your summer already. They offer a TON of options to choose from, like Keto and vegetarian, so you can pick a plan that fits your lifestyle. I recently tried their garlic and herb chicken breast and it was so tasty. Without fail their meals are FLAVORFUL, NEVER DRY, and consistently really delicious. That’s why I keep working with them. Head to factor75 dot com or click the link below and use code LEEJA50 to get 50% off your first Factor box! Thanks Factor! After Nixon was elected president in 1968, the Committee to Re-Elect the President, or CRP for short, was founded as a fundraising committee to help secure Nixon’s 1972 re-election campaign. But that committee, well they were willing to do a lot more than silly little fundraising events and voter registration drives to try to get their man re-elected. No no, they wanted to commit crimes. B&Es. Wiretapping. Espionage. Covert surveillance. They wanted to be secret spy agents. And so they made a plan. They would break into the Democratic National Committee headquarters, take pictures of important documents, and plant wire taps so they could gather information that would help them win the 1972 election for Republican Richard Nixon. In january 1972, Gordon Liddy, the finance counsel for the CRP presented a campaign intelligence plan to CRP leadership, including former Atty General John Mitchell, The plan included illegal activities against the democratic party. After some revisions, Mitchell and other CRP leaders approved the plan. By May 1972, the CRP started executing their plan. They paid burglars who broke into DNC headquarters and wiretapped 2 phones, including DNC chairman Larry O’Brien. Not long after, however, the committee discovered that the listening devices they had planted in the DNC needed repairs, so the group again planned to break into DNC headquarters to do those repairs and take some more pictures. This time, however, they got caught. The immediate fallout from the attempted burglary and subsequent arrests was… subdued. Nixon played dumb. His press secretary called it nothing more than a “third-rate burglary attempt.” At an August 29 press conference, Nixon stated "I can say categorically that ... no one in the White House staff, no one in this Administration, presently employed, was involved in this very bizarre incident." Behind the scenes, however, the coverup had already begun. Martha Mitchelll, the wife of former Attorney General John Mitchell who was, you’ll recall, the head of the CRP, made the connection that the CRP and her husband knew the men who had been arrested for the burglary and were likely behind the whole incident. She tried to speak to the press. 5 days after the break in, Martha was on the phone with a reporter saying she would leave her husband if he didn’t quit the dirty business of politics when the conversation ended abruptly, and Martha could be heard in the background telling someone to get away from her. Then, the line went dead. A former FBI agent had ripped the cord out of the wall and forcibly restrained her. She would be held captive for five days and forcibly sedated to keep her from sharing what she knew with the media. During this time the FBI was investigating the Watergate incident, and a mysterious FBI source, nicknamed Deep Throat was feeding Washington Post reporters information about what the FBI was finding. On august 1 the FBI found that a $25,000 cashiers check (worth $175,000 in today’s money) was deposited into the bank accounts of one of the men arrested at Watergate that night. The kicker? That check was from a private individual and made out to the Committee to Re-Elect the President. How did a check meant for Nixon’s re-election campaign end up in the bank account of the unknown burglar who had then used that money to buy the hardware used to break into the DNC?? THE PLOT THICKENS. On October 10, less than one month before the 1972 election, Bob Woodward and Carl Bernstein of The Washington Post reported that the FBI had determined that the Watergate break-in was part of a massive campaign of political spying and sabotage on behalf of the Nixon re-election committee. The Nixon campaign was immediately on the defensive, endlessly attacking the Washington Post for its disinformation and campaign of fake news against the President. The Washington Post reporters Bob Woodward and Carl Bernstein were relentlessly dedicated to uncovering the scandal, and they had a hunch that something big was brewing. The rest of the media, however, either downplayed it or failed entirely to cover the story at all. In the meantime, Nixon had a huge re-election budget, solid approval ratings, and recent success opening China to the world with a visit that year, as well as achieving detente with the Soviet Union, successes that were used to drown out calls by the Washington Post that something was amiss. Less than a month after the Washington Post revealed that Watergate was connected to Nixon’s re-election campaign and administration, Nixon won the 1972 presidential election in one of the biggest landslides in American political history. In January, 1973, right before Nixon’s inauguration for his second term, the trial began against the seven men accused of participating in and orchestrating the Watergate break in. Five of the men, including CIA operative E. Howard Hunt, plead guilty. Gordon Liddy and James McCord refused to co-operate and they were convicted. Ol James McCord, though, he had a change of heart. So before sentencing, he wrote the judge a letter, saying officials “high up” in the White House had orchestrated the plot and had pressured the defendants to plead guilty and take the fall. When McCord’s letter to the judge was released, it sent ripples through the country. What the Washington Post reporters had been screaming for months became incredibly apparent: the Watergate break in was just the tip of the iceberg. This wasn’t a simple criminal case. This was a political scandal that quickly captured national attention. In May of 1973, the Senate officially created the Watergate Committee to investigate the CRP, the President, and the lead up and subsequent cover up of the Watergate break in. You know the January 6th committee and all those televised hearings they held? Yeah the Watergate Committee was like that. The DOJ launched its own independent investigation, led by special counsel Archibald Cox. Consider him the Bob Mueller or John Smith of the Watergate scandal. Ah how history loves to repeat itself. And if you remember the January 6th committee hearings, well the Watergate hearings were like that on steroids. They were broadcast on every major news network throughout the summer of 1973, and over 70% of American households with TVs tuned in to watch as the hearings unfolded. The bombshell came on Monday, July 16th, when White House assistant Alexander Butterfield appeared to testify. With the revelation that Nixon had been recording everything said inside the Oval Office since 1970, the nature of the investigation changed. It became focused intensely on gaining access to those tapes to determine definitively whether Nixon was involved in the Watergate plot, and what he did to cover it up. Cox immediately subpoenaed the tapes as part of the DOJ investigation, as did the Senate Watergate committee, but Nixon refused to comply. Doesn’t look good, Richie. His reason for why he didn’t have to comply with the subpoena? Executive privilege. Sound familiar? 2 weeks later, the judge involved with the Watergate investigation ordered Nixon to hand over 9 tapes for private review. And 2 months later, in October 1973, Nixon’s vice president, Spiro Agnew, resigned amid charges of receiving thousands of dollars in kick backs and other improper payments throughout his political career and into his Vice Presidency. Nixon named then-House Minority Leader Gerald Ford as his new Vice President 2 days later. Meanwhile, Nixon continued fighting the subpoena for his tapes in court. After the appeals court ruled that, no, executive privilege didn’t apply, and yes he would have to hand over those tapes, Nixon began feeling the walls closing in on him. In a panic, on October 20th, 1973, Nixon asked Cox, the special investigator trying to get his hands on those tapes, to resign. Cox refused. Nixon then asked attorney general Elliot Richardson to fire Cox. Richardson refused, and in protest he resigned his post as Attorney General. So then Nixon ordered Richardson’s assistant, William Ruckelshaus, to fire Cox. He also refused and then resigned. Next in line was Solicitor General Robert Bork, and he finally was like fine I’ll do it, and Bork finally fired Cox, putting an end to the night history would call The Saturday Night Massacre. Less than a month later, in November 1973, Nixon urged the nation to just get over Watergate already, uttering what would become his most infamous line “I’m not a crook.” That same week, the White House told the Senate and the DOJ special Watergate investigation that oops! We can’t find 2 of those tapes you asked for. And also oops! One of the tapes has 18 and a half minutes of silence right in the middle. We don’t know how that happened???????? After a year of investigations, in March of 1974, a grand jury in Washington DC voted to indict seven former aides of President Nixon for conspiring to hinder the Watergate investigation, including former attorney general John Mitchell, you know the one who had his own wife held hostage so she wouldn’t tell the media. John Mitchell as attorney general was known for his gruffness and strong tough on crime, law and order stance. So it was incredibly ironic that he was indicted and later sent to prison for obstruction of justice, conspiracy, and perjury in relation to his planning and cover up of the Watergate scandal. In that same indictment, Nixon was secretly named an unindicted co-conspirator, under the belief that he couldn’t be formally indicted while he was a sitting president, even though they had lots and lots of evidence to believe he was definitely guilty of some crimes. After this, things quickly began unraveling for Nixon. In april 1974, the DOJ subpoenaed 64 more Nixon tapes. In May, the House Judiciary Committee began hearings on whether to write up articles of impeachment. In July, the Supreme Court definitively ruled that Nixon’s tapes were not protected by executive privilege. By the end of July, the House Judiciary Committee adopted three articles of impeachment against the President for obstructing the Watergate investigation, misuse of power and violating his oath of office, and failure to comply with House subpoenas. Then, on August 5th, 1974, having lost his executive privilege argument at the Supreme Court, Nixon released transcripts of 3 conversations he had six days after the Watergate break in. These transcripts would become known as the smoking gun of the watergate investigation. They showed Nixon ordering the FBI to stop its investigation into the break in, and directing a cover up of the entire scandal. 3 days later, before formal impeachment proceedings could begin, Nixon stunned the nation in a televised address by announcing that he would be resigning the Presidency, effective at noon the next day. The next day, on August 9, 1974, Vice President Gerald Ford, who 10 months earlier had just been the minority leader in the house of representatives, became the 38th President of the United States. A month later, President Gerald Ford granted Nixon a full, free, and absolute pardon for all offenses against the United States. Nixon maintained his innocence until his death in 1994. Overall, 69 people were indicted and 48 people were convicted for participating in or covering up the Watergate break in. Republicans lost tons of seats in the 1974 midterm elections as cynicism and distrust of government increased from the Watergate incident, combined with fallout from the failures of the Vietnam War. Congress made moves to significantly limit the power of the executive branch, including passage of the Privacy Act of 1974 which established fair information practices related to the collection and use of information about individuals maintained by federal agencies like the FBI. The Church Committee in the Senate held hearings to unearth other covert government activities and domestic spying, uncovering evidence that the CIA had spied on domestic political opponents, carried out assassinations overseas, and experimented with LSD on unwitting Americans. These revelations further eroded the nation’s trust in government. The Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act in 1978 created tight controls for CIA wiretaps and other investigation methods, and created a stronger system of checks and balances to mitigate against future amassing of power by the CIA director. Most law schools around the country instituted a policy requiring ethics courses and the American Bar Associated created a stronger professional responsibility code after Watergate, because nearly half of the people indicted during the scandal were lawyers. In other reforms, caps were placed on campaign contributions, transparency laws were strengthened, and new laws codified how an independent counsel would investigate future presidents. This string of reforms gave more power to Congress and took it away from the presidency, in order to try to curb any future executive misbehavior and avoid a similar constitutional crisis. Despite these reforms, Watergate did irreparable damage to America’s collective faith in government, how we think about the Presidency, and how we view politicians. Nixon’s successors Ford and Carter were unable to secure second terms. Then came Reagan in the 1980s, and he brought back some of that shiny veneer to the presidency, despite the fact that he did ruin everythin[b]g. And in the decades since Watergate, many of those reforms have been rolled back. Court rulings set campaign financing restrictions back. Surveillance increased dramatically after the 9/11 attacks. Trump was able to fire inspectors general with no legal consequences. And the independent counsel statute expired in 1999, meaning that Trump’s own attorney generals were able to appoint special counsels, which is exactly what post-Watergate reforms were meant to avoid. Trump for his part seems to be well aware of the Watergate scandal, despite the fact that I’m not certain he’s literate, but because 50 years have passed since the scandal erupted, and today we generally have a pretty tenuous collective grasp on the details and what the fallout of Watergate was, Trump has been able to rewrite the history of Watergate in a way that speaks volumes about his opinion on how much impunity with which a President should be able to act. In the early days of his candidacy for President, Trump pointed to Hilary’s private email server as “Magnitudes worse than Watergate.” This despite the fact that Trump openly embraced many Nixonisms, including his Law & Order campaign rhetoric from 1968. Trump’s campaign chairman Paul Manafort confirmed that Trump would base his nomination acceptance speech on the one Nixon used in 1968. And in tweets and speeches throughout his campaign and presidency, Trump repurposed Nixon quotes and phrases, including using terms like lawlessness, and echoing Nixon’s fears about a “frightening trend of crime and anarchy” especially during the protests that erupted after the murder of George Floyd here in Minneapolis, not to mention that his obsession with discrediting the mainstream news media closely mirrors the ways in which Nixon attempted to discredit the media, especially during the Watergate investigations. And this isn’t hypothetical, Trump literally knew Nixon. He was close with Nixon advisor Roy Cohn and friends with Nixon’s Treasury secretary John B. Connally. Trump and Nixon spent a weekend together in 1989 celebrating Connally’s birthday, sharing dinner together and riding back to New York on Trump’s private jet together. Nixon wrote Trump a letter, saying Nixon’s wife had predicted that “whenever you decide to run for office you will be a winner.” All of this makes Trump’s cognitive dissonance around the Watergate scandal all the more puzzling. Hillary’s emails are worse than Watergate. Nixon is someone to be venerated and who’s political style Trump is mimicking in many ways. But also according to Trump Nixon may not have been guilty and was a coward for quitting. “He left. I didn’t leave.” Trump said in 2019. “Big difference. I don’t leave.” Basically, Nixon screwed up. He got caught. Meanwhile, people on the left hurl accusations that Trump’s actions are worse than or akin to Watergate, like for instance when he fired FBI director James Comey, asked the FBI to stop investigating his national security adviser Michael Flynn, or tried to interfere with Robert Mueller’s investigation, or withheld troops while urging Ukraine to investigate his political opponent Joe Biden. But Trump’s retelling of Watergate has sown enough seeds of doubt that he’s able to manipulate history for his supporters, spinning it into another example of how there are conspiracies at every turn meant to take powerful people (like Trump) down. Which is the same rhetoric Trump turns to when any accusation of wrongdoing is thrown his way, from sexual misconduct to high crimes and misdemeanors. According to historian Michael Gerhardt, “Truth doesn’t matter, what matters is the narrative. It’s much more important what people believe than what they know.” By changing the Watergate narrative, Trump has opportunities to manipulate history, and his lawyers have opportunities to go to court and relitigate the same questions that Nixon faced and lost. The Supreme Court found against Nixon then, but maybe they got it wrong. Or that’s what Trump and his lawyers want to argue. Trump doesn’t have to respond to subpoenas or submit to investigations. The president can bypass Congress, open DOJ cases and investigations against his enemies, and authorize the assassination of an Iranian commander without telling Congress, etc etc etc. During the FBI search of his Mar A Lago home, Trump claimed it was a Watergate-style violation, which is a strange and inaccurate comparison. According to a recent Politico article, “If everything is “worse than Watergate,” Watergate becomes run-of-the-mill political intrigue, not the archetype of presidential corruption. It creates,“a kind of numbing effect.” A numbing effect Trump can use to normalize his own behavior.” And the two men mirror each other in many other ways. Nixon was the first criminal president, and the first president to resign the post. Trump is the first former president to be arrested and face federal charges. Both seemed to have a general lack of regard for democratic processes and a will to gain power no matter what it takes. Both exhibit extreme paranoia and insecurity and isolationism. Nixon purposely organized his schedule so he would interact with as few people as possible. Trump famously spent hours everyday alone watching cable news. Both repeatedly railed against the news media, especially at the height of investigations into them. The thing that’s different now is that Republicans then were more willing to believe facts. Today, the facts themselves are up for debate, leading to partisan chaos and a Republican party that paradoxically follows Trump while also shouting for law and order. And of course the increased trend of social media siloing people based on their political party and feeding the masses disinformation, means more than ever we do not trust our government, but depending on your political position the reasons for this are very very different. And when a former President, seeking re-election, is spouting disinformation at every opportunity, and that same disinformation has uprooted an entire party’s grasp on reality, it becomes harder and harder to tell fact from fiction. And the result is an entire electorate that distrusts the government, doesn’t believe in the democratic process, and feels more than ever that the system is rigged against them. If you liked this video and want to learn more, I suggest watching my video from last week, all about why Republicans fall for fake news. Thank you to my Patreon supporters, including my newest patron, and an extra special shout out to my multip-platinum patron Brett Piontek. If you’re interested in behind the scenes content, access to my research and show notes, content about my dog, and all sorts of other stuff, consider joining me over on Patreon today. Thanks so much for watching have a good day BYE

**Structure:** Opens with a minute-by-minute narrative of security guard Frank Wills discovering the taped door latches at the Watergate complex on June 17, 1972, then a sponsor read (Factor, a prepared-meal service), then a historical walkthrough of the scandal and its lasting institutional effects, closing with a cross-promo to her fake-news video and the standard Patreon sign-off.
**Framing:** True-crime narrative cold open (a specific person, date, and physical detail) used to launch a historical explainer, the same structural device as the police and segregation videos in this batch, applied here to a 1970s scandal instead of a modern incident.

### 21. Why Do Conservatives Hate Feminism? (2023-08-23) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=enwpRTNctek)

The light is non radiating, convenient, safe, and efficient. It converts light energy to heat to treat each area. I really like Kenzzi for hair removal because I’ve tried pretty much everything else. Again, I really like the glide mode where you just hold down the button for 5 seconds and then it starts continually pulsing. Super easy.
